## Page 1


Engineering topological flat bands in Γ-valley moiré with Ising-type SOC: twisted
1T–ZrS2 and 1T–SnSe2
Hanqi Pi,1, ∗Yves H. Kwan,2, 3, ∗Haoyu Hu,1, 4, 5 Yi Jiang,1 Dumitru Călugăru,6 Jie Shan,7, 8, 9, 10 Kin Fai
Mak,7, 8, 9, 10 Miguel M. Ugeda,1, 11 Dmitri K. Efetov,12, 13 Maia G. Vergniory,1, 14 and B. Andrei Bernevig4, 1, 11, †
1Donostia International Physics Center (DIPC), Paseo Manuel de Lardizábal. 20018, San Sebastián, Spain
2Department of Physics, University of Texas at Dallas, Richardson, Texas 75080, USA
3Princeton Center for Theoretical Science, Princeton University, Princeton, NJ 08544
4Department of Physics, Princeton University, Princeton, NJ 08544, USA
5Department of Physics, University of Science and Technology of China, Hefei, Anhui 230026, China
6Rudolf Peierls Centre for Theoretical Physics, University of Oxford, Oxford OX1 3PU, United Kingdom
7School of Applied and Engineering Physics, Cornell University, Ithaca, NY 14850, USA
8Department of Physics, Cornell University, Ithaca, NY 14850, USA
9Kavli Institute at Cornell for Nanoscale Science, Ithaca, NY 14850, USA
10Max Planck Institute for the Structure and Dynamics of Matter, Hamburg, Germany
11IKERBASQUE, Basque Foundation for Science, Bilbao, Spain
12Faculty of Physics, Ludwig-Maximilians-University Munich, Munich 80799, Germany

Ludwig-Maximilians-University Munich, Munich 80799, Germany
14Département de Physique et Institut Quantique, Université de Sherbrooke, Sherbrooke, J1K 2R1 Québec, Canada
Twisted moiré superlattices hosting topological flat bands provide a platform to explore the inter-
play between topology and correlations. Here we investigate topological band structures in Γ-valley
moiré systems based on 1T–ZrS2 and 1T–SnSe2. Using large-scale ab initio calculations and contin-
uum modelling, we demonstrate that both materials exhibit an approximate spin-U(1) symmetry
and host isolated topological moiré valence bands, including quantum spin Hall and high spin Chern
states. By constructing a hierarchy of Γ-valley moiré continuum models, we show that isolated moiré
bands carry a trivial C3 symmetry indicator when the low-energy physics is described by a single
effective orbital and a single layer-hybridized branch (either bonding or antibonding). Topological
bands therefore arise from the inter-branch and/or inter-orbital coupling. Moreover, we determine
the interaction-driven phase diagrams using Hartree–Fock and exact diagonalization, finding vari-
ous phases tunable by twist angle, interaction strength, and displacement field. We identify specific
conditions under which fractional Chern insulators are favored. Together with previous work show-
ing that the moiré conduction bands of 1T–ZrS2 and 1T–SnSe2 realize M-valley twisting and host
quasi-one-dimensional physics [1], our results establish these systems as ideal platforms for strongly
correlated moiré physics and provide a systematic framework for understanding topological band
structures in Γ-valley moiré materials.
I.
INTRODUCTION
Moiré superlattices have become a highly tunable plat-
form for exploring correlated and topological quantum
matter, with bandwidth, correlations, and topology con-
trolled by twist angle, displacement field, and carrier
density [2–7].
Experiments have realized a wide vari-
ety of correlated phases, including unconventional su-
perconductivity in graphene moiré systems [8–16], as
well as Mott insulators, generalized Wigner crystals [17–
21], and superconductivity [22–24] in TMD moiré het-
erostructures.
The nontrivial band topology of moiré
flat bands further intertwines with correlations [25–28]
to produce phenomena such as the fractional Chern in-
sulators (FCIs) at zero magnetic field [29–37] and the
fractional quantum spin Hall insulator [38] observed in
twisted MoTe2, although more work is needed to deter-
mine the origin of experimental observation. Exploring
∗These authors contributed equally to this work.
† bernevig@princeton.edu
and understanding new moiré platforms that naturally
host isolated topological flat bands is a key step toward
robustly engineering these exotic correlated and topo-
logical quantum phases.
Research has extended moiré
physics beyond the familiar K-valley to platforms whose
low-energy states reside at other momenta and in distinct
symmetry environments [39–43]. These include Γ-valley
dominated regimes in TMD homobilayers and twisted
double bilayers [44–49], as well as M-valley systems that
realize quasi-one-dimensional or highly anisotropic tri-
angular/rectangular moiré lattices [1, 50, 51]. Although
these non-K-valley platforms broaden the accessible cor-
relation physics, the single-particle moiré bands are often
topologically trivial [44, 49]. This motivates the search
for new moiré materials that intrinsically host topological
minibands. In the weak-coupling regime, several works
have proposed systematic theoretical frameworks based
on symmetry-based continuum modelling and topological
quantum chemistry to predict and diagnose topological
moiré flat bands [52–55].
In this work, we use twisted bilayer 1T-ZrS2 and 1T-
SnSe2 as concrete material platforms and study the ori-

gin of topological moiré bands in Γ-valley moiré system.
Both systems host topological moiré valence bands, in-
cluding quantum spin Hall (QSH) and high-spin Chern
states, as established by large-scale density functional
theory (DFT) calculations and continuum modelling.
The QSH state in AB-stacked twisted 1T-ZrS2 has been
reported in Ref. [42], where the topology was attributed
to strong spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and an emergent
kagome lattice.
Here we study both AA- and AB-
stacked twisted 1T-ZrS2 and 1T-SnSe2 and develop a
symmetry-based microscopic framework to understand
isolated topological moiré bands in systems with Ising-
type SOC. Based on the symmetry and orbital analy-
sis of the monolayer, we identify the origin of an ap-
proximate spin-U(1) symmetry in the low-energy states
of twisted bilayer ZrS2 and SnSe2.
Because Γ-valley
states in the two layers remain momentum-aligned un-
der twisting, interlayer tunneling is generally strong and
naturally forms layer-hybridized bonding and antibond-
ing branches. Hereafter, “branch” refers to these bond-
ing/antibonding layer-hybridized states.
By analyzing
a hierarchy of symmetry-constrained continuum models,
we show that isolated topological bands in spin-U(1)
-symmetric moiré systems require inter-branch and/or
inter-orbital coupling. Finally, using Hartree–Fock and
exact diagonalization (ED), we observe spin-polarized
Chern states and QSH states, and discuss the conditions
required to realize FCIs in these platforms. Our frame-
work provides a symmetry- and material-based route
to systematically analyze and design topological moiré
bands based on 1T-TMDs, which can be straightfor-
wardly generalized to other moiré and superlattice plat-
forms.
II.
TOPOLOGICAL MOIRÉ BANDS IN
TWISTED BILAYER ZRS2 AND SNSE2
We first examine the monolayer band structures of
1T–ZrS2 and 1T–SnSe2.
Both materials crystallize in
a hexagonal lattice as shown in Fig. 1(a) and belong to
the space group (SG) P3m1 (No. 164). In this work,
we focus on the valence bands, and the analysis of the
conduction bands can be found in Ref. [1].
As shown
in Figs. 1(b) and 1(c), the monolayer valence band max-
ima (VBM) of both compounds lie at Γ (in SnSe2, SOC
shifts the VBM from the Γ–M line to Γ), and are domi-
nated by chalcogen (px, py) orbitals. Crucially, the top-
most state at Γ transforms according to a 2D irreducible
representation (IRREP) without SOC, and the inclu-
sion of SOC splits it into two Kramers pairs. Although
both compounds have the same crystalline symmetry and
chalcogen (px, py)-dominated Γ-valley valence states, the
two Kramers pairs are assigned to different IRREPs. In

Γ9 with odd parity under inversion, while in SnSe2 they
transform as Γ4Γ5 and Γ8 with even parity.
The IR-
REP notation follows the convention of Bilbao Crystal-
lographic Server (BCS) [56–58]. The Γ6Γ7 (Γ4Γ5) pair
corresponds to p-like effective orbital with total angular
momentum |Jz| =
3
2 and Γ9 (Γ8) exhibits s-like char-
acter with |Jz| = 1
2. The different IRREPs in the two
compounds can be understood from the fact that the Γ-
valley states arise from the hybridization between (px, py)
orbitals on the two chalcogen sublayers through the inter-
mediate metal sublayer, which forms combinations with
even- and odd-parity since the two sublayers are related
by I. Because the chalcogen orbitals hybridize with dif-
ferent metal-orbitals, i.e., Zr-d in ZrS2 and Sn-s in SnSe2,
the relative ordering of these two combinations can be
reversed, leading to different IRREP assignments for the
topmost Kramers pairs.
The orbital component at Γ plays a central role in de-
termining the effective spin symmetry of the low-energy
moiré bands. Depending on the orbital character, the re-
sulting moiré low-energy states can have either approx-
imate spin-U(1) or spin-SU(2) symmetry.
To see this,
we project the onsite atomic SOC, HSOC = λL · S =
λ
2 (2LzSz + L+S−+ L−S+), onto the low-energy (px, py)
manifold. Introducing the circular basis |p±⟩= |px⟩±i|py⟩
√
2
for the topmost Γ-state without SOC, the ladder opera-
tors L± connect the low-energy p± sector to the higher-
lying pz sector and therefore have no matrix elements
within the projected subspace.
Thus, within the low-
energy (px, py) manifold, the SOC is diagonal in Sz and
is therefore Ising-like, which splits the doubly degener-
ate states without SOC into two spinful Kramers dou-
blets as shown in bottom panel of Fig. 1(d).
By con-
trast, if the low-energy Γ-valley manifold is dominated
by an mℓ= 0 orbital such as dz2 in MoTe2, the projected
first-order SOC vanishes and spin anisotropy arises only
through virtual mixing with remote bands. This is the
origin of the approximate spin-SU(2) symmetry of the
Γ-valley moiré bands in bilayer and multilayer twisted
MoTe2 [49, 59]. In the exact spin-SU(2) limit with time-
reversal symmetry, the Z2 index is trivial.
Because the monolayer structure lacks the C2z sym-
metry, the untwisted bilayer structure allows for two dis-
tinct configurations. The first, referred to as AA stack-
ing, is constructed by placing one monolayer directly on
top of the other and preserves the symmetry of the mono-
layer i.e., P3m1 (No. 164). The second, referred to as


## Page 4

AB stacking, is obtained by applying C2z to the bottom
layer and belongs to SG P6m2 (No. 187). Upon twist-
ing, the inversion symmetry I (or in-plane mirror Mz)
present in the untwisted AA (AB) configuration is bro-
ken, while the C3z and in-plane C2 about [010] ([210])
are preserved. As shown in Figs. 2(a) to 2(c), the large-
scale DFT calculations at twist angle 3.89◦show that
AB–SnSe2 hosts a single isolated set of bands composed
of the top two spinful valence bands. By contrast, AA-
SnSe2 does not exhibit an isolated valence-band manifold
for θ ≥3.89◦(see Fig. S8), and we therefore do not fur-
ther discuss it. Twisted AA– and AB–ZrS2 instead or-
ganize the top six spinful bands into three isolated sets.
In all cases, the isolated band sets exhibit approximately
double degeneracy along high-symmetry lines.
By in-
specting the DFT wavefunctions, we find that spin-U(1)
remains an excellent approximate symmetry for these
low-energy isolated bands, with symmetry breaking be-
low 2% according to the measure defined in Eq. (S1.3).
This approximate symmetry is inherited from the mono-
layer Γ-valley states discussed above. Therefore, we can
diagnose the topology from the spin Chern number Cs de-
fined as Cs = ln

Parameter values are listed in Tables S4 and S5. These
reduced models make the emergent approximate sym-
metries transparent.
Twisted AB SnSe2 has an addi-
tional inversion I in the simplified continuum model,
which is a zero-twist symmetry [1] and originates from
Mz of the untwisted AB-stacked bilayer as proved in
Section II 2 a. For twisted AB-ZrS2, the zero-twist sym-
metry remains inversion-like in its action on moiré co-
ordinates, but its representation in the orbital subspace
is no longer that of a pure inversion.
Because the re-
tained s-like and p-like orbitals have the same inversion
parity, a pure inversion would act trivially, whereas they
are distinguished through different Jz. The action on or-
bital subspace of zero-twist symmetry is therefore equiv-
alent, up to an overall phase, to that of C2z, so the sim-
plified model of twisted AB-ZrS2 exhibits emergent C2z
instead of I.
Besides, the simplified Hamiltonians for
twisted AA- and AB-stacked ZrS2 are related by the frac-
tional translation Tτ0 = {E|τ0} with τ0 = 2

where aM,1 and aM,2 are moiré lattice vectors. This re-
lation is clearly reflected in the LDOS patterns shown in
Figs. 2(g) and 2(h) and leads to a corresponding phase
shift of the C3z eigenvalues at KM/K′
M, as discussed
in Section III 3 b.
The origin of Tτ0 = {E|τ0} can be
understood from the interlayer-distance patterns of re-
laxed twisted structure at 3.89◦. Strictly speaking, the
AA and AB corrugation fields cannot be exactly related
by a moiré translation, since the two stackings have dif-
ferent symmetries. However, if we quantify the similar-

5
ity between the two corrugation patterns by the Pear-
son correlation coefficient defined in Eq. (S2.75), which
is computed between the relaxed interlayer-distance field
in AB-case dAB(r) and AA-case dAA(r + τ0), we obtain
ρ = 0.93.
This value shows that the two corrugation
fields are close to being Tτ0-related in the first-harmonic
simplified model, and in turn motivates the emergent ap-
proximate symmetry ˜C2z = {C2z|τ0} in twisted AA ZrS2.
These emergent symmetries, I and C2z/ ˜C2z, in addition
to the spin-U(1) symmetry, account for the near-double
degeneracy observed in the low-energy moiré bands of
twisted AB-stacked SnSe2 and AA/AB-stacked ZrS2 in
Figs. 2(a) to 2(c).
III.
ENGINEERING TOPOLOGICAL FLAT
BAND IN Γ-VALLEY
Building on the results for twisted bilayer SnSe2 and
ZrS2, we now investigate the microscopic origin of their
band topology and derive the condition for isolated topo-
logical bands in spin-U(1) -symmetric Γ-valley moiré sys-
tems. Closely related symmetry-based analyses were de-
veloped in Ref. [52, 54], where the topology of the low-
energy moiré bands is inferred from the monolayer sym-
metry group, the monolayer IRREP at Γ, and the moiré
symmetry group. For the moiré systems with P31m or
P3m1 symmetry group that is constructed by monolayer
system with C3z symmetry, an isolated topological insu-
lating phase is symmetry-enforced by the band represen-
tation when the relevant monolayer Γ state transforms
as ¯Γ4¯Γ5. Nonetheless, due to the absence of symmetry
indicator in the moiré symmetry group, the band repre-
sentation analysis is not able to distinguish fragile from
stable topology. In our case, the approximate spin-U(1)
symmetry inherited from the monolayer allows us to dis-
tinguish the stable topology from the fragile topology
through the C3z symmetry indicator [60]. Besides, the
spin-U(1) symmetry forbids the isolated moiré bands to
have stable topology in the single-orbital-single-branch
limit as shown below.
We begin with the simplest single-orbital-single-branch
limit with C3 symmetry, in which the low-energy Hilbert
space is described by a single effective orbital with total
angular momentum Jz = ±mJ and a single interlayer-
hybridized branch (either layer bonding or antibonding).
At the moiré ΓM point, we assume the moiré poten-
tial is not strong enough to induce level crossings. Thus
the topmost Kramers pair is adiabatically connected to
the topmost Kramers pair of the untwisted multilayer at
Γ, and its C3z eigenvalues are ξΓM,s = e−i 2π
3 σmJ with
σ = +1 for s =↑and σ = −1 for s =↓. To determine Cs,
we next analyze the moiré KM and K′
M points. In the
continuum description, a basis state at fixed moiré crys-
tal momentum k is denoted by |k, Q, s⟩, where Q is a
moiré reciprocal-lattice vector and the physical momen-
tum is k −Q. In the single-orbital-single-branch limit,
the six spinful low-energy states at KM are described by
the three basis states,

ing, it has C2y with x and y axes defined in Fig. 1(a).
As a result, the bands along this line are spin de-

M splits

symmetry, the upper isolated doublet obtained from this
splitting necessarily carries a nonzero spin Chern num-
ber, and therefore realizes a stable topological band, as
shown in the right panel of Fig. 3(c).

constrained by Eq. (3). We need to include multiple or-
bitals or multiple layer-hybridized branches to break the
constraint and obtain isolated band with stable topol-

orbital-single-branch case is analogous and given in Sec-

and the topmost Kramers-pair band is isolated from

Hence the isolated band pair is topological precisely
when the highest KM block in the two spin sectors car-
ries different Πz. A topological transition occurs when,
upon varying model parameters, the highest KM block
in one spin sector exchanges with another block carry-
ing a different Πz, as shown in Fig. 3(d). In the first-
harmonic continuum models, the energies of these blocks
are controlled by the competition between splitting re-
sulted from interlayer hybridization and moiré potential.
Consequently, pressure or changes of twist angle might
modify the relative strength between moiré potential and
interlayer coupling. Such tuning can therefore drive the
required band inversion and change Cs by ±1.
The topological bands found in twisted bilayer SnSe2
and ZrS2 can be understood within this framework. In
the cases where the interlayer distance is minimized
on the honeycomb sites, the single-orbital-single-branch
limit produces a Dirac-like manifold at the top of the va-
lence spectrum. Inter-orbital or inter-branch couplings
then gap and split this connected manifold, producing
the isolated topological band pairs observed in the con-
tinuum model and DFT calculations. Because twisted
AA-stacked ZrS2 at 3.89◦is approximately related to the
twisted AB-stacked ZrS2 by Tτ0, the moiré potential max-
ima also form a honeycomb lattice, except that they oc-
cupy the 1a Wyckoff position and one of the honeycomb
sites, as shown by the LDOS distribution in Fig. 2(g).
Consequently, the topological bands are again engineered
by the splitting induced by inter-orbital coupling.
IV.
HARTREE-FOCK AND
EXACT-DIAGONALIZATION RESULTS
We now analyze the interacting phases of AA- and AB-
stacked twisted bilayer ZrS2 and AB-stacked twisted bi-
layer SnSe2 using self-consistent Hartree-Fock and ED.
Experimentally, accessing these hole fillings in the va-
lence moiré bands requires carrier densities of order
1013 cm−2 to 1014 cm−2, which is within reach of ionic
gating via the electric double layer it forms at the surface
(see Section I 4). We start from the spin-U(1) symmet-
ric continuum model and include density-density interac-
tions described by a dual-gate screened Coulomb poten-
tial V (q) =
e2
2ϵ0ϵr|q| tanh(|q|dsc), with dielectric constant
ϵr setting the effective interaction strength. In HF calcu-
lations, we project to the top four moiré bands per spin,
and freeze all lower remote bands to be filled. We enforce
moiré translation symmetry with the possible exception
of inter-spin coherence (ISC) at wavevector q correspond-
ing to spontaneous breaking of U(1) symmetry. We con-
strain q to the C3z-symmetric momenta ΓM, KM, K′
M.
More details of the implementation are given in Sec-
tion IV.
Fig. 4 summarizes HF phase diagrams as a function
of interaction strength 10/ϵr and twist angle θ at integer
fillings ν. For both stackings of twisted bilayer ZrS2, a
spin-polarized Chern insulator with C = 1 at ν = −1
appears only beyond a critical interaction strength as
shown in Figs. 4(a) and 4(b). For weaker interactions,
the system remains metallic.
The critical interaction
strength grows with θ, consistent with the increasing
noninteracting bandwidth of the top valence band. In
AB-stacked SnSe2, the first moiré valence band is nar-
rower than in twisted ZrS2, and therefore the insulating
C = 1 phase at ν = −1 emerges at interaction strengths
as weak as 10/ϵr = 0.4, as shown in Fig. 4(c). Further-
more, at stronger interaction strengths, both SnSe2 and
ZrS2 undergo a transition from the C = 1 insulator to
a C = 0 insulator at ν = −1. Due to the indirect en-
ergy overlap between the first and second single-particle
moiré valence band within each spin sector, a finite in-
teraction strength is required to open a gap in both AA-
and AB-stacked ZrS2 at ν = −2, as shown in Figs. 4(d)
and 4(e). Nonetheless, the critical interaction strength
for obtaining insulating phases is smaller than at ν = −1
for the same twist angle.
For example, in AB-stacked
ZrS2 at 5◦, a gap opens only for 10/εr > 2.0 at ν = −1,
whereas it already opens for 10/εr > 1.2 at ν = −2.
Close to the metal-insulator boundary in both AA- and
AB-stacked ZrS2, the gapped phase is a time-reversal-
symmetric topological phase: either a quantum spin Hall
state with spin Chern number C = 1 (QSH1) or a Z2
topological insulator with weak ISC. At stronger inter-
actions, the system develops sizable ISC and the result-
ing insulator has C = 0 for AA stacking or C = 1 for
AB stacking. In SnSe2 at ν = −2, we do not observe
a stable QSH1 or Z2 topological insulator phase for the
interaction strengths 10/ϵr ≥0.4 explored here due to
strong ISC and band inversion. Nonetheless, in an inter-
mediate twist angle region 3.2◦< θ < 5.2◦, we observe a
C = 1 Chern insulator with sufficient interaction strength
as shown in Fig. 4(f). We also study the effect of a dis-
placement field D on twisted bilayer ZrS2 at θ = 3.89◦
as shown in Fig. S28.
The displacement field tunes a
topological transition between C = 1 and C = 0 spin-
polarized insulators at ν = −1, and it suppresses the
QSH1/Z2 topological phase in favor of a time-reversal-

with finite ISC marked by dashed outlines, “Z2 TI” denotes a topological insulator with broken spin-U(1) symmetry, in contrast
to the QSH1 phase which preserves spin-U(1) . Panels (a, d) correspond to AA-stacked ZrS2, (b, e) to AB-stacked ZrS2, and

found in Section IV.
To assess the potential for fractional Chern insulators
(FCIs) [29–31, 63–65], we complement HF with exact
diagonalization (ED) calculations.
We focus on AA-
stacked ZrS2 at θ = 3.89◦and consider ν = −1/3 and
ν = −2/3 restricted to the topmost valence band within
one spin sector.
Similar results hold for AB-ZrS2 and
AB-SnSe2 with twist angles ranging from 2◦to 6◦. In
the HF calculation for AA-stacked ZrS2 at θ = 4◦, we
observe that the bandwidth of the unoccupied HF band
nearly doubles for the C = 1 state at ν = −1 [Fig. S27],
driven by the Fock self-energy difference between ΓM
and KM. To offset the interaction-induced broadening
and explore flatter effective bands, we introduce an ar-
tificial scaling factor αkin on the non-interacting disper-
sion, E(k) →αkinE(k), with αkin = 1 corresponding to
the physical band, αkin = 0 to a perfectly flat band, and
αkin < 0 to an inverted dispersion. We diagnose FCIs by
identifying the putative topological ground-state mani-
fold, which consists of the three lowest states with mo-
menta consistent with the FCI momenta [29, 66]. The
spread of the lowest states should be smaller than the
gap between the three lowest states and the lowest state
not in this manifold. As shown in Figs. 4(g) and 4(h), ro-
bust FCI signatures at ν = −1/3 and ν = −2/3 appear
only within a window of negative αkin.
To rationalize
this, we estimate an “effective” band dispersion,
Eeff(k) = αkinE(k) + |ν| EHF,int(k),
(6)
where EHF,int(k) is the interaction-induced HF con-
tribution for a fully hole-occupied valence band [see
Eq. (S4.6)], rescaled by |ν| to mimic fractional fillings.
The effective bandwidth extracted from Eeff(k) exhibits a
minimum as a function of αkin that closely coincides with
the FCI region identified by ED as shown in Figs. 4(i)
and 4(j). This indicates that, for the physical dispersion
(αkin = 1), interaction-induced band broadening drives
the system away from the optimal flat-band limit, and
FCIs are only stabilized when the bare dispersion is in-
verted to partially compensate the broadening. The ap-
pearance of a C = 1 state at ν = −1 is consistent with
Ref. [42]. However, we do not find an FCI at ν = −1/3
without artificially inverting the band dispersion. This
difference is likely related to the ordering of the second
and third sets of single-particle bands, which depends on
the choice of van der Waals correction. A detailed com-
parison is provided in Section I 3.
V.
DISCUSSION
By combining large-scale DFT calculations with faith-
ful continuum models, we establish twisted bilayer ZrS2


## Page 9


9
and SnSe2 as concrete Γ-valley platforms that host iso-
lated topological moiré bands at small twist angles. Our
analysis identifies the key ingredients for such bands
in spin-U(1) -symmetric Γ-valley moiré systems. First,
the relevant monolayer state near the band edge should
transform as a two-dimensional IRREP without SOC,
corresponding to effective orbitals with ml ̸= 0. This re-
duces the approximate spin symmetry of the low-energy
moiré bands from spin-SU(2) to spin-U(1) .
Second,
the moiré problem must go beyond the single-orbital-
single-branch limit where isolated topological bands
are symmetry-constrained to be trivial.
Inter-orbital
or inter-branch couplings generate isolated topological
bands either by splitting Dirac-like bands in layer group
p321 or by driving band inversions in layer group p312,
as confirmed by the simplified models for AB-stacked
SnSe2 and AA/AB-stacked ZrS2.
Analysis of the sim-
plified models for these materials validates this frame-
work.
Moreover, the competition between bandwidth
and interaction yields a rich phase diagram. In both ZrS2
and SnSe2 systems, Hartree–Fock calculations find spin-
polarized Chern insulators at ν = −1, and symmetry-
preserving QSH1/Z2 phases at ν = −2, with the ability
to tune Chern numbers and close gaps via a displacement
field.
Exact diagonalization suggests that interaction-
induced Fock broadening currently disfavors FCIs.
As
a route to stabilize FCIs, future work may explore en-
hancing dielectric screening asymmetrically or leverag-
ing substrate-induced moiré potentials to quench the ki-
netic energy further, thereby realizing the full potential
of these Γ-valley platforms.

FIG. S5. The monolayer crystal structure of SnSe2 and ZrS2 and corresponding band structure. (a) and (b) show monolayer
crystal structure of SnSe2 and ZrS2 from the top view and side view, respectively. The monolayer band structure with SOC of
(c) SnSe2 and (d) ZrS2 with p-orbital projection of Se and S atoms, respectively. The red and green colors indicate the orbital
weight of px,y and pz, respectively. The enlarge monolayer band structure of (e) SnSe2 and (f) ZrS2 without (black lines) and
with (red lines) spin-orbit coupling. We label the double irreducible representation (IRREP) of the two topmost bands at the
Γ point.
In this section, we present ab initio results for the monolayer and untwisted bilayer structure of SnSe2 and ZrS2.
As shown in Figs. S5(a) and S5(b), the monolayer crystal structures of both materials have Shubnikov Space Group
(SSG) 164.86 P¯3m11′ symmetry, characterized by symmetry generators C3z, C2 with rotated axes along [100] direction,
inversion symmetry I and time-reversal symmetry T . The hexagonal lattice vectors are given by
a1 = a (1, 0) ,

For SnSe2 and ZrS2, the lattice constant and out-of-plane displacement of Se or S atoms are a = 3.811 Å, z =
1.528 Å [67] and a = 3.661 Å, z = 1.441 Å [68], respectively. The Sn or Zr atom is located at the 1a Wyckoff position
featuring ¯3m site symmetry, while the Se or S atoms are positioned at 2d Wyckoff position and have 3m symmetry.
Within a unit cell, the two 2d Wyckoff positions are mapped to one another by inversion.
We focus on the valence bands since the conduction bands have been fully studied in [1].
The valence band
structures of monolayer SnSe2 and ZrS2 with SOC is shown in Figs. S5(c) and S5(d). Due to the presence of PT
symmetry, every band in both ZrS2 and SnSe2 is doubly degenerate across the whole Brillouin Zone (BZ). In both
materials, the Γ valley is dominated by the (px, py) orbitals of chalcogenide atoms. A comparison of the low-energy
bands without and with SOC is shown in Figs. S5(e) and S5(f). Without SOC included, the valence band maxima
(VBM) of ZrS2 are located at Γ while the VBM of SnSe2 is located on Γ −M path that is near Γ. As shown by
the red lines, SOC lifts the twofold degeneracy (without spin degree of freedom) of the topmost state at Γ point in

FIG. S6. The crystal and band structure for untwisted AA/AB-stacking bilayer SnSe2 and ZrS2. (a) and (d) show the AA-
and AB-stacked bilayer crystal structures, respectively. (b) and (e) show the band structure for AA- and AB-stacked untwisted
bilayer SnSe2, respectively. (c) and (f) show the band structure for AA- and AB-stacked untwisted bilayer ZrS2, respectively.
The black and red colors denote the band structures without and with spin-orbital coupling included.
both SnSe2 and ZrS2. In SnSe2, SOC further shifts the VBM from a high symmetry line to the Γ point. Because Se
atom is heavier than the S atom, we can find the SOC splitting, defined as the separation between the two spinful
Kramers doublets obtained from the spinless twofold-degenerate state (or fourfold with spin) at Γ, in SnSe2(278 meV)
is much stronger than that in ZrS2(83 meV). The first and second topmost double-degenerated bands at Γ in ZrS2
transform as the Γ6Γ7 and Γ9 double irreducible representations (IRREPs) in the spinful case, while the first and
second topmost double-degenerated bands at Γ in SnSe2 transform as the Γ4Γ5 and Γ8 double IRREPs. Although
both monolayers share the same lattice symmetry and have Γ-valley valence states dominated by chalcogen (px, py)
orbitals, the corresponding Γ-point IRREP is not fixed by orbital character alone. Because the two chalcogen atoms in
one monolayer unit cell are related by inversion, the (px, py) manifold forms even- and odd-parity layer combinations,
and the ordering of these two doublets depends on their hybridization with nearby metal-derived states. Here the
two compounds differ qualitatively: in ZrS2 the low-energy conduction bands is mainly Zr-4d, while in SnSe2 it is
mainly Sn/Se-(s, p) derived. This difference changes how the even- and odd-parity chalcogen (px, py) combinations
are renormalized, and hence leads to different parity ordering at Γ.
By stacking one monolayer directly on top of another, we obtain the AA-stacked bilayer as shown in Fig. S6(a).
The symmetry group of the AA-stacked bilayer is the same as the monolayer case, i.e., SSG 164.86 P¯3m11′. The
band structure for AA-stacked SnSe2 and ZrS2 are shown in Figs. S6(b) and S6(c). Because PT symmetry is present,
all bands are doubly degenerate throughout the Brillouin zone. Comparing the monolayer band structures without
SOC in Figs. S5(e) and S5(f) with the bilayer band structures without SOC in Figs. S6(b) and S6(c), we find that
interlayer coupling splits the topmost monolayer Γ state into the top two interlayer bonding and antibonding states
at bilayer Γ. The corresponding interlayer splitting, defined as the bonding–antibonding splitting of the topmost Γ
state, is 90 meV and 74 meV in SnSe2 and ZrS2, respectively. As in the monolayer, including SOC shifts the VBM of
SnSe2 from a high-symmetry line to Γ and opens gaps at high-symmetry points/lines. Notably, in ZrS2 the SOC and
interlayer energy scales are comparable, making the ordering of topmost 8 spinful states at Γ sensitive to the choice
of van der Waals (vdW) corrections (see detailed discussion in Section I 3).
Because the monolayer structure lacks C2z symmetry, we can apply C2z to the bottom layer of AA-stacked bilayer
and obtain the AB-stacked bilayer as seen in Fig. S6(d). The AB-stacked bilayer has P¯6m21′ (SSG 187.210) symmetry
which is generated by C3z, vertical mirror M100, horizontal mirror Mz and T . The head-to-head configuration of AB-
stacking results in larger interlayer distances (7.016 Å for SnSe2 and 6.362 Å for ZrS2) compared to the AA-stacking

configuration (6.166 Å for SnSe2 and 5.866 Å for ZrS2). Here, the interlayer distance is defined as the distance between

2.
Twisted AA/AB-stacked bilayer
By rotating the top layer relative to the bottom one, we obtain the AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer structures
at a series of angles. We take the convention that ˆy direction aligns with the [010] direction as shown in Fig. S7,
following Ref. [1]. The twisted AA-stacked bilayer SnSe2 and ZrS2 break I symmetry in the untwist configuration and
belong to SSG P3121′ (No. 149.22) which is characterized by symmetry generators C3z, C2x, T , and translational
symmetry TR, as shown in Fig. S7(a).
The twisted AB-stacked bilayer SnSe2 and ZrS2 break Mz symmetry in
the untwist configuration and belong to SSG P3211′ (No.
150.26) with symmetry generators C3z, C2y, T , and
translational symmetry TR, as shown in Fig. S7(b). We first study the moiré band structures using large-scale DFT
calculation (the M-valley conduction bands are detailed in Ref. [1]). The valence bands for twisted SnSe2 (Fig. S8)
and ZrS2 (Fig. S9) are shown for AA (top rows) and AB (bottom rows) from 9.43◦to 3.89◦. At 3.89◦, the top two
spinful bands in AB-SnSe2 form a single isolated set. We don’t discuss the top isolated band in AA-SnSe2 since the
direct gap between the second and third spinful band is too small. In AA/AB-ZrS2 at 3.89◦, the top six spinful
bands organize into three isolated sets of two spinful bands each. Moreover, the bands are nearly doubly degenerate
along high-symmetry lines. As shown in Section II 3, this degeneracy arises from zero-twist inversion I in twisted
AB-stacked structure and from the ˜C2z symmetry in twisted AA-ZrS2.
Moreover, by inspecting the DFT wavefunction along high-symmetry lines, we find that the isolated bands in both
SnSe2 and ZrS2 remain nearly eigenstates of sz, which is the Pauli matrix acting in spin space and trivially on all
other degrees of freedom. We define spin-U(1) symmetry as [H(k), sz] = 0 and quantify its breaking by

3.
Influence of vdW correction
In Section I 1, we mentioned that the energy scale of interlayer splitting (74 meV in AA-stacking and 57 meV in
AB-stacking) and SOC energy splitting (83 meV) is comparable in ZrS2. Because the interlayer coupling strength
is strongly related to the interlayer distance, the band structures of both untwisted and twisted bilayer ZrS2 are
sensitive to the choice of vdW. To illustrate this, we relaxed untwisted AA–ZrS2 using Grimme DFT-D3 (D3-vdW)
and Tkatchenko–Scheffler (TS-vdW) corrections. Bilayer structure relaxed by D3-vdW has a larger interlayer dis-
tance 5.866 Å than the one relaxed by TS-vdW 5.683 Å. Consequently, TS-relaxed structure shows a larger splitting
(105 meV) than the D3-relaxed one (74 meV) due to the smaller interlayer distance as shown in Fig. S10(a). Moreover,
the interlayer splitting is slightly larger than the SOC splitting, whereas in the TS-relaxed structure, the SOC splitting

is slightly larger than the interlayer splitting. Consequently, the inclusion of SOC leads to a different ordering of layer
hybridized antibonding and bonding states at Γ. Specifically, in the TS-relaxed structure, the top two states at the Γ
point are both layer-hybridized antibonding states (¯Γ6¯Γ7 and ¯Γ9) as shown in Fig. S10(b). These two states arise from
the interlayer hybridization and subsequent splitting of the corresponding monolayer ¯Γ6¯Γ7 and ¯Γ9 states (Fig. S5(f)).
In contrast, in the D3-relaxed structure, the top two states are antibonding and bonding states (¯Γ6¯Γ7 and ¯Γ4¯Γ5,
respectively as shown in Fig. S10(b)) that originates solely from the interlayer-hybridization-induced splitting of the
monolayer ¯Γ6¯Γ7 state. The band order swap is corroborated by the symmetry representations at Γ, as shown in
Fig. S10(b). The different ordering is inherited by the twisted bilayer, as shown in Fig. S9 (relaxed by D3-vdW) and
in Ref. [42] (relaxed by TS-vdW).
4.
Ionic Gating of Two-Dimensional Semiconductors
For many two-dimensional semiconductors to be experimentally useful, it is crucial to gate them efficiently from
their intrinsic semiconducting gap into the conduction or valence band, so that measurable electron or hole transport
can be achieved. Ionic gating is particularly well suited for this task because it forms an electric double layer at
the surface, acting like an ultra-thin capacitor with a very large capacitance (typically ∼1 µF cm−2 to 50 µF cm−2),
far exceeding that of conventional oxide gates.
This allows carrier density changes on the order of 1013 cm−2 to
1014 cm−2, sufficient to move the Fermi level by ∼0.4 eV to 0.8 eV and drive the system from the gap deep into either
the electron or hole bands. Such densities are high enough not only to enable ambipolar transport in materials like
transition metal dichalcogenides, but in some cases to induce metallic or even superconducting behavior. Depending
on the application, ionic gating can be implemented using liquid ionic electrolytes, ion gels, polymer electrolytes, or
solid-state ionic conductors, offering a versatile platform for strong and reversible electrostatic doping of 2D crystals.


## Page 18


18
Ionic gating has been demonstrated to induce the required carrier densities in closely related 2D materials, including
monolayer and few-layer transition metal dichalcogenides [69–71] and twisted bilayer graphene-based moiré systems.
At low temperatures, the ionic liquid is frozen and the induced charge is fixed, making ionic gating compatible with
the transport and spectroscopic probes needed to resolve the Chern-insulator and QSH phases predicted in Section IV.
Combined with a conventional back gate, ionic gating also allows independent tuning of the carrier density and the
out-of-plane displacement field, enabling the displacement-field-driven topological transitions discussed in Fig. S28.
Appendix II: Continuum model
To elucidate the topology and related physics in twisted bilayer ZrS2 and SnSe2, we construct continuum models
following Refs. [1, 61]. We briefly summarize the method here, and full details can be found in those references.
We first perform Wannier and valley projection of the DFT Hamiltonian to retain only the target orbitals and
valleys, yielding a reduced DFT Hamiltonian. We then parameterize a symmetry-constrained continuum model and
determine the coefficients of the symmetry-allowed terms by linear least-squares fitting to the reduced Hamiltonian,
as in Refs. [1, 61]. The obtained model reproduces the low-energy band structure and wavefunctions with numerical
accuracy. Nonetheless, this model can still contain many terms that hinder analytical understanding. Therefore,
we further simplify the model by combining the nonlinear fitting with the step-wise regression procedure proposed
in Ref. [1].
Finally, we can obtain a model with far fewer parameters that still captures the low-energy physics
quantitatively.
1.
Reduced DFT Hamiltonian obtained from projection method
In this section, we summarize the DFT calculation and the projection setup used to obtain the reduced DFT
Hamiltonian, following the notation of Ref.[1]. The full Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian is obtained from the OpenMX [72, 73]
package, which is expressed in the non-orthonormal pseudo-atomic orbital (PAO) basis. We denote the PAO basis
as |φilαs (RM + τilα)⟩, where RM denotes moiré cell, l labels the layer, i indexes the monolayer unit cell within the
moiré supercell, α labels the orbital, and s is the spin. For ZrS2, we use Zr-s2p2d2 and S-s2p2d2 PAO basis set. For
SnSe2, we use Sn-s2p2d2f1 and Se-s2p2d2 PAO basis set.
The Wannier projection procedure is inspired by the construction of Wannier functions [74–77]. In this step, our goal
is to construct a set of orthonormal Wannier bases that faithfully spans the isolated low-energy subspace. To this end,
we first analyze the orbital component of the isolated low-energy valence bands. As shown in Figs. S5(c) and S5(d),
the top 12 valence bands are separated from the rest and dominated by p-orbitals of chalcogen atoms in both ZrS2
and SnSe2. Accordingly, for each monolayer unit cell we choose three p-orbitals on the two S/Se sites as the projected
trial states |ϕilαs (RM + τilα)⟩, which gives 1 ≤α ≤6. We then project these trial states onto the isolated low-
energy subspace A, spanned by the Kohn-Sham eigenstates {|ψn⟩}n∈A, through the projector ˆPA = P
n∈A |ψn⟩⟨ψn|.
The projected trial states are thus defined as

In the following section, we derive the corresponding representation matrices D(g), taking into
account both the exact symmetries of the twisted bilayer and the additional constraints inherited from the untwisted
configuration [1], which we refer to as zero-twist symmetries.
a.
Zero-twist symmetry of Γ-valley from local stacking approximation

Because H(∆R) retains the lattice translation symmetry of a monolayer, Sl1α1s1;l2α2s2(∆R, r) depends only on the
relative coordinate r, not on the absolute positions of the two sites. Moreover, translating one layer by a primitive
lattice vector leaves the local stacking unchanged, so
Sl1α1s1;l2α2s2(∆R + ai, r) = Sl1α1s1;l2α2s2(∆R, r),

c.
Exact and zero-twist symmetry constraints in twisted bilayer SnSe2
The exact and zero-twist symmetries in twisted SnSe2 are identical to those in ZrS2. However, in SnSe2, we keep
only the ¯P orbital in our continuum model basis since the s molecular orbital basis only contributes to deeper moiré
bands as discussed in Section II 1. So we drop the orbital index α in SnSe2. Besides, unlike ZrS2 where P and ¯s are
inversion-odd,molecular orbitals R in SnSe2 have even parity under inversion. Consequently, although the symmetry

differ. For twisted AA-stacked SnSe2, the continuum basis ˆc†
l,s is

t,↑, ˆc†
t,↓, ˆc†
b,↑, ˆc†
b,↓},
(S2.61)
the representation matrices reads,
DAB-SnSe2(C3z) = τ0 ⊗s0,
DAB-SnSe2(C2x) = τx ⊗−isx,
DAB-SnSe2(T ) = τ0 ⊗−isyκ,
DAB-SnSe2(Mz) = τx ⊗−isz.
(S2.62)
In AB-stacked case, the representation matrices for C2y and I are,
DAB-SnSe2(C2y) = τx ⊗isy,
DAB-SnSe2(I) = τx ⊗−is0.
(S2.63)
3.
Continuum model extracted/fitted from ab initio Hamiltonian
In the above section, we outlined the symmetry constraints and representation matrices for the continuum basis
ˆc†
l,α,s in twisted AA/AB-stacked ZrS2 and SnSe2. By applying constraints of exact and/or zero-twist symmetries, we
obtain the independent symmetry-allowed terms of hQi,Qj(k). Since the projected DFT Hamiltonian (hDFT(k) in
Eq. (S2.4)) is equivalent to the symmetry-constrained continuum model in the continuum limit, we can parametrize
the continuum Hamiltonian in terms of the independent symmetry-allowed terms and extract the numerical values of
their coefficients from the projected DFT Hamiltonian hDFT(k) [1, 61]. We built two models of different complexity.
First, we construct a continuum model with dozens to hundreds of parameters that reproduces both band dispersions
and wavefunctions of the top six/two spinful bands in ZrS2/SnSe2. To assess the wavefunction agreement, we define
the overlap between the DFT wavefunction |ψdft⟩and the continuum model wavefunction |ψmodel⟩. Note that because
the isolated low-energy bands are almost doubly degenerate, each pair of nearly-degenerate bands is grouped into one
set. The overlap for m-set of bands at k is defined as follows,
Om(k) =
r
Tr [Pm,DFTPm,model]
2
,
Pm,DFT/model =
X
i∈m
|ψi,DFT/model⟩⟨ψi,DFT/model|.
(S2.64)
Here, Pm,dft/model is the projector defined using the two degenerated bands in set m. The full models achieve overlaps
above 99% and 95% for ZrS2 and SnSe2, respectively. Second, to gain analytical insight, we apply the step-wise
regression procedure of Ref. [1] to reduce parameters of the full model and obtain a simplified model with less than 10
parameters (including effective mass). We start from the continuum model with dozens to hundreds of parameters,
and test the effect of removing each parameter individually, while keeping the others fixed. The parameter whose
removal increases the loss the most is discarded, and the reduced model is then refit.
Repeating this procedure
produces a nested set of models with progressively fewer parameters. We then choose the simplest model that still
achieves the desired accuracy. The simplified model still matches the dispersions and wavefunctions of isolated bands,
with overlaps exceeding 93% and 75% for ZrS2 and SnSe2, respectively. In what follows, we detail both continuum
models for each material.
a.
ZrS2
For ZrS2 system, we consider up to first harmonic (|Qi −Qj| = |bM1|) for k-independent terms ( nx + ny = 0),
and Qi = Qj for the k-dependent terms (i.e., 1 ≤nx + ny ≤2). Because the model contains two layers, two orbitals
(P and ¯s molecular orbitals) and two spin flavors, the first-harmonic continuum Hamiltonians constrained by the
exact symmetries involve 65 and 61 independent terms for twisted AA-stacked and AB-stacked ZrS2, respectively, as
listed in Tables S9 and S10. We extract the parameters by linear least-squares fitting and compare the full continuum
model with the projected DFT Hamiltonian in Fig. S12 from twist angles 5.09◦to 3.89◦(AA on the top row, AB
on the bottom row). The dispersions match closely and the wavefunction overlap exceeds 99% for the top three sets
of bands. Besides, the C3 eigenvalues of the continuum model at C3 invariant high symmetry points agree with the
DFT results shown in Table S2 and Table S3.

FIG. S12. The comparison of band structure between the first-harmonic continuum model and DFT for twisted ZrS2. (a-c)
Band structure for twisted AA-stacked ZrS2 from 5.09◦to 3.89◦. (d-f) Band structure for twisted AB-stacked ZrS2 from 5.09◦
to 3.89◦. The black line is the result of the first-harmonic continuum model, and the red dots are DFT results with color
intensity reflecting the overlap between the DFT and continuum-model wavefunctions.
For the first-harmonic model with 65 and 61 parameters for twisted AA- and AB-stacked ZrS2, we calculate the
Wilson loops for the top three sets of bands at 3.89◦. As shown in Figs. S13(a) and S13(g), the first set of bands
has a nontrivial winding number and exhibits a quantum spin Hall (QSH) state for both stackings. On the contrary,
both the second and third sets of band have trivial Z2 indices as shown in Figs. S13(b), S13(c), S13(h) and S13(i).

We next examine the real-space charge distribution of the isolated bands. The local density of states (LDOS) for
the top three sets in both AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer ZrS2 are shown in Fig. S14. The hexagon marks the
moiré Wigner–Seitz cell, the two arrows indicate the moiré lattice vectors, and color encodes intensity from blue (low)
to red (high). The LDOS distribution exhibit the crystalline symmetry for both stackings: (a) within each layer,
the LDOS in both AA-stacked and AB-stacked ZrS2 show the C3z symmetry, (b) the top layer (first row) is related
to the bottom layer (second row) by the exact symmetry C2x and C2y in AA-stacked and AB-stacked configuration,
respectively. In Fig. S14(a), the LDOS of the topmost set of band is relatively extended with minimum at the 1b
Wyckoff position. The second set of band is maximized at 1b Wyckoff position and forms a triangular lattice as
illustrated in Fig. S14(b). The third set exhibits a kagome pattern with sites in the middle between the 1a and 1c
Wyckoff positions, as depicted in Fig. S14(c). Furthermore, the AA and AB LDOS are related by a rigid fractional
moiré translation: shifting the AB LDOS by 2
3aM,1 + 1
3aM,2 maps the patterns of the top three sets in AB onto those
in AA as shown by Figs. S14(d) to S14(f). Finally, the LDOS patterns display approximate twofold symmetry about
z. More specifically, LDOS in AA are nearly invariant under C2z operation with in-plane fractional translation, i.e.,
˜C2z = {C2z | ( 2
3, 1
3)}, whereas in AB they are nearly invariant under C2z. Neither of them is an exact symmetry of
the system.

AA and AB structures share essentially the same leading moiré corrugation texture up to a shifted origin, while
the residual field measures the remaining higher-harmonic and relaxation-induced corrections, so the τ0 relation is
emergent rather than exact.


## Page 33


33
b.
AB-stacked SnSe2
For the numerically exact model of twisted AB-stacked SnSe2 at 4.41◦, we include up to the second harmonic for the
k-independent and linear terms (0 ≤nx +ny ≤1, |Qi −Qj| ≤|bM1 +bM2|) and restrict to Qi = Qj for the quadratic
terms (nx + ny = 2). Imposing the exact symmetries yields 61 independent terms as listed in Table S11. We fit their
coefficients to the reduced DFT Hamiltonian and compare the resulting continuum model with DFT in Fig. S18(a).
The top two spinful bands of the model track the DFT dispersions, and the minimum wavefunction overlap exceeds
93%. The C3 eigenvalues at C3-invariant momenta also match the DFT results in Section I 2. Note that we restrict
to the top two spinful bands when extracting parameters from DFT reduced Hamiltonian.
Higher-energy moiré
bands receive sizable weight from a monolayer subvalley near Γ along the Γ−M line, i.e., a non–high-symmetry-point
(NHSP)s valley (See Figs. S6(b) and S6(e)). Because this NHSP valley lies close in energy to Γ-valley, folding the
monolayer bands into the moiré Brillouin zone at 4.41◦produces extra low-energy bands in SnSe2 that are absent
in folded ZrS2 at 3.89◦(Figs. S18(b) and S18(c)). These additional bands originate from the NHSP subvalley, drive
strong deviations from a simple parabolic kinetic approximation, and make stable parameter extraction difficult if
more bands are included.
We evaluate the LDOS of the topmost band set for the full model (Fig. S18(d)). The top- and bottom-layer LDOS
form rings around the 1b and 1c positions, respectively. They are related by C2y and also exhibit an approximate
inversion (I) symmetry in addition to the exact generators. To determine the topology, we compute the Wilson loop
(the left of Fig. S18(e)), which shows a nontrivial winding as in AB–ZrS2. Given the approximate spin-U(1) symmetry
present both in the projected DFT Hamiltonian and full continuum model, we also compute spin-projected Wilson
loops (the right of Fig. S18(e)). The spin-up and spin-down sectors have winding numbers +1 and −1, consistent
with the C3 eigenvalues in Section I 2. The spin-projected Berry curvature for the top band set (middle and right of
Fig. S18(f)) is concentrated near KM line and nearly cancels between spins, as seen in the total Berry curvature (left
of Fig. S18(f)).
To gain a deep understanding of the system, we reduce the number of parameters and obtain a simplified analytic
model. Inspecting the projected DFT Hamiltonian, we find the zero-twist inversion symmetry holds to within about
1% for the top band set, which explains the additional I observed in the LDOS of the full model. We therefore impose
I together with the approximate spin-U(1) symmetry, and drop the k-dependent moiré potential terms. With these
simplifications, the continuum model for SnSe2 takes the form
hAB(r) = −ℏ2∇2

Here, we use Vi and wi to denote the interlayer and intralayer coupling parameters, respectively. τi and si (i = 0, x, y, z)
are identity/Pauli matrices acting in layer and spin subspaces, respectively. The first- and second-harmonic moiré

3z bM,1 and g2i = Ci−1

listed in Table S5. Because of the nearby monolayer NHSP valley, the k-dependent moiré terms are essential in SnSe2.
Omitting them causes substantial renormalization of the fitted parameters in the simplified model compared to the full
continuum model. To assess the fidelity of the simplified model, we compare the dispersion with DFT and compute
wavefunction overlap. As shown in Fig. S19(a), the top band set reproduces the overall dispersion, and the overlaps
exceed 75% along high-symmetry lines. The regular and spin-projected Wilson loops are shown in Fig. S19(b), which
match those of the full model. Fig. S19(c) show the LDOS of the simplified model, which differs slightly in detail but
retains the ring-like weight around the 1b and 1c positions. Besides, LDOS of top and bottom layers are related by
both the exact symmetry C2y and zero-twist symmetry I. The spin-projected Berry curvature for the topmost band
set (Fig. S19(d)) has equal magnitude and opposite sign in the two spin channels. The approximate combined IT
symmetry accounts for both the near double degeneracy of the DFT isolated band set and the nearly vanishing total
Berry curvature.

Appendix III: Topological property of Γ-valley systems
The isolated topological moiré bands observed in twisted bilayer SnSe2 and ZrS2 motivate us to examine the origin
of their topology.
We start from the monolayer band structure and identify which features are prerequisites for
nontrivial moiré topology. Two closely related symmetry-based analysis studies were developed in Ref. [52, 54], where
the topology of the low-energy moiré bands is inferred from the atomic symmetry group, the monolayer IRREP
at Γ, and the moiré symmetry group. In our case, the presence of spin-U(1) symmetry in moiré system inherited
from the monolayer provides additional information but also imposes an additional constraint. One the one hand,
spin-U(1) symmetry allows us to distinguish the stable topology from the fragile topology through the C3z symmetry
indicator [60]. On the other hand, the spin-U(1) symmetry applies additional constraints to the conditions of isolated
topological moiré band as shown below.
1.
Approximate spin-U(1) symmetry in D3d/D3h monolayer Γ-valley
In this section, we explore the origin of approximate spin-U(1) symmetry and spin-SU(2) symmetry observed in
Γ-valley moiré bands based on twisted bilayer/multilayer 1H-phase MoTe2[49, 59] and twisted bilayer 1T-ZrS2/SnSe2,
respectively.
We first analyze the orbital component dominating band edge at monolayer Γ in these three materials, which
can be inferred from crystal-field splitting together with the metal electron configuration. In 1H-phase as shown in
Fig. S20(a), the metal atoms reside in a trigonal-prismatic crystal field, causing the d orbitals to split into a singlet a′
1
(dz2) and two doublets, namely e′ (dx2−y2, dxy) and e′′ (dxz, dyz) [79]. Because orbitals with lobes pointing along the
metal–chalcogen bonds are pushed to higher energies by Coulomb repulsion, the energy ordering is Ee′′ > Ee′ > Ea′
1.
Because 1H-MoTe2 is composed of group-VI transition metals, where Mo donates 4 electrons and has effectively d2
shell, only the lowest a′-state is occupied while the other states are unfilled. Consequently, in MoTe2 (and other
group-VI TMDs), the valence band edge at Γ are typically dominated by the dz2 orbital, while conduction band
edge at Γ have predominantly e′-type character hybridized with in-plane chalcogen p orbitals [80–82]. In 1T phase
as shown in Fig. S20(b), the metal atoms occupy octahedral sites. In the local octahedral frame the d orbitals split
into t2g (dx′y′, dx′z′, dy′z′) and eg (dz′2, dx′2−y′2) manifolds, where Eeg > Et2g. Note that we use x′y′z′, where z′
is pointing from the metal atom to one of the chalcogen atom, to distinguish the xyz used in the following global
framework. In the ideal 1T-phase monolayer with trigonal distortion, the t2g manifold further rehybridizes into an
a1g singlet and an eπ
g doublet, which in the global hexagonal frame can be viewed as dz2-like and (dx2−y2, dxy)-like
states, respectively [79, 83]. For 1T-phase composed by the group-IV metal such as ZrS2, the metal is nominally in a
d0 configuration. Therefore, the valence bands is dominated by filled chalcogen p orbitals and the conduction band is
dominated by empty metal d-orbitals [84, 85]. Note that SnSe2 is composed of the group-14 main-group element Sn,
which is formally in a 4d10 configuration. The filled 4d shell lies deep in energy and is chemically inert. Consequently,
the conduction band states are dominated by antibonding hybridization between Sn 5s and Se 4p states, while the
valence-band edge retain predominantly chalcogen p character. In both ZrS2 and SnSe2, the valence band edge at Γ
is then dominated by a (px, py) doublet as shown in Fig. S5.
The little group of the monolayer Γ point is D3h (generated by C3z, C2x, Mz, and T ) for the 1H phase, and D3d
(generated by C3z, C2x, I, and T ) for the 1T phase. For the two point groups, the IRREPs in the non-SOC case and
their orbital basis functions are listed in Table S6. From the discussion above, band-edge states at Γ dominated by
out-of-plane orbitals (e.g., dz2 in valence band edge of MoTe2) transform as one-dimensional IRREPs, whereas states


## Page 36


36
dominated by in-plane orbitals (e.g., (px, py) in ZrS2 and SnSe2) transform as two-dimensional IRREPs. We consider
the atomic SOC interaction,
HSOC = λ L · S = λ

LzSz + 1
2
 L+S−+ L−S+

,
(S3.1)
with L± = Lx ±iLy. For bands transforming as 1D IRREPs, e.g., valence band edge at Γ in 1H-MoTe2, the dominant
orbital component is dz2 has ml = 0. In this case Lz|ml = 0⟩= 0, and the ladder operators L± connect the low-energy
orbital to higher-lying |ml = ±1⟩states, so SOC only enters the low-energy subspace in second order. As a result,
the low-energy states retain an approximate spin-SU(2) symmetry.
By contrast, orbitals forming 2D IRREPs have nonzero magnetic quantum numbers (ml = ±1 for (px, py) and
ml = ±2 for (dx2−y2, dxy)). In the D3d case relevant to 1T-TMDs, the low-energy doublet at Γ is contributed by
either the chalcogen (px, py) orbitals in the valence band or the metal (dx2−y2, dxy) orbitals in the conduction band.
Projecting HSOC onto this isolated two-dimensional IRREP without SOC, only the diagonal LzSz term survives
to leading order and yields an Ising-type SOC that splits.
In D3h, where inversion symmetry is absent, metal
(dx2−y2, dxy) and chalcogen (px, py) basis states can mix within the same 2D IRREP. Nonetheless, the horizontal
mirror Mz constrains the projected SOC on E′-IRREP manifold to be Ising-like and supports an emergent spin-U(1)
conservation in the low-energy continuum model. Crucially, these SU(2)-breaking (often effectively U(1) -preserving)
SOC terms are inherited by the moiré continuum model and can facilitate the formation of isolated topological bands
upon twisting.
From this analysis, we propose that the presence of a two-dimensional IRREP without SOC at Γ is a key ingredient
for realizing nontrivial Γ-valley moiré bands. Because monolayer and untwisted bilayer calculations are significantly
less computationally expensive than fully relaxed twisted-bilayer simulations, this criterion provides a practical pre-
screening tool to identify candidate materials capable of hosting topological moiré physics.

2.
U(1) -symmetric Γ-valley moire systems with different symmetry space groups
Based on the above discussion, we now analyze the topology of a Γ-valley moiré system with spin-U(1) inherited
from the monolayer. The twisted bilayer belongs to the layer group p321 or p312 with T , depending on whether it is
in the AA- or AB-stacked configuration. A generic twisted multilayer configuration belongs to the layer group p3. In
the presence of spin-U(1) symmetry, the topology of an isolated band can be diagnosed from the spin Chern number
Cs using Eq. (S1.4), which is determined by the C3 eigenvalues ξk,s at k ∈{ΓM, KM, K′
M} with s =↑, ↓. We assume
that turning on the moiré potential does not result in level crossing at ΓM, so the Kramers pair at moiré ΓM nearest
to the charge neutrality is adiabatically connected to the one of the untwisted multilayer at Γ. If this local Kramers
doublet has quantum number Jz = ±mJ for the total angular momentum, its C3 eigenvalue are ξΓM,↑= e−i 2π
3 mJ and
ξΓM,↓= ei 2π
3 mJ. In the following sections, we discuss the possible value of C3 eigenvalues at at KM and K′
M under
different conditions for different layer groups, and hence determine the topology of the system. Finally, because of
the strong interlayer coupling in Γ-valley systems, we transform from the top/bottom layer basis, denoted by l, to the
layer-hybridized bonding/antibonding basis, denoted by λ = ±1.

Therefore, the two spinful doublets of j-th state Eq. (S3.22) and of j′-th state in Eq. (S3.24) must be degenerate, and
together form a fourfold degenerate state at KM.
As shown in Eqs. (S3.20) and (S3.21), the ordering between the split twofold-degenerate state and the fourfold-
degenerate state is determined by the sign of V1. When V1 < 0, the fourfold-degenerate state lies higher in energy,
whereas when V1 > 0, the twofold-degenerate state lies higher. This can be understood from the real-space structure
of the moiré potential together with the elementary band representations (EBRs) of layer group p321 (No. 68)
with T , listed in Table S7. For the first-harmonic moiré potential in Eq. (S3.17), when V1 < 0, the potential is

maximized at the 2c position. The topmost band set can then be understood as placing an s-like (p-like) orbital
with |mJ| = 1/2 (|mJ| = 3/2) at 2c, which yields two copies of a spinless Dirac band as shown in Fig. S22(c). The
corresponding band representation is 1¯E2¯E@2c (¯E¯E@2c), and the fourfold-degenerate state at KM carries ξKM,s =

twofold-degenerate state at KM carries ξKM,s = e−i 2π
3 σmJ. In moiré systems, we assume that the strength of the
moiré potential can be roughly reflected by the spatial variation of the interlayer hybridization. Regions with smaller
interlayer distance typically have stronger interlayer hybridization and hence correspond to maxima of the effective
moiré potential. For twisted bilayer AB-stacked ZrS2 and SnSe2, which belong to layer group p321, the interlayer
distance is minimized at the 2c position as shown in Fig.S11 and Fig.S16 in Ref.[1]. Consequently, in both materials
the topmost band set is expected to originate from the Dirac-like band.

So far the discussion has been restricted to the single-orbital-single-branch limit, where every isolated band set is
topologically trivial and equivalent to an EBR. In realistic systems this limit is not always sufficient, and one may need
to include both layer-hybridized branches or more than one orbital. Such inter-branch or inter-orbital coupling acts as
an effective “SOC” term that breaks the emergent ¯C2y symmetry. We first consider the single-branch limit containing
two effective orbitals. Without loss of generality, we assume the two effective orbitals originate from monolayer (px, py)
and are characterized by total angular momentum Jz = ±mJ with mJ = −1
2 (i.e., p−,↑and p+,↓) and mJ = 3

Thus, once either the two-orbital terms in Eq. (S3.27) or the two-branch terms in Eq. (S3.30) are included, the
emergent ¯C2y symmetry is lost while the exact C2y and T symmetries remain. Consequently, the additional same-spin
doubling is lifted, but the exact C2y symmetry still enforces spin degeneracy at each valley. Therefore the fourfold
multiplet of the SBSO limit splits into two spinful doublets, namely Eq. (S3.22) and Eq. (S3.24).
Time-reversal
symmetry T maps each spinful doublet at KM to a spinful doublet at K′
M with the same unordered C3 content.
Hence an isolated split doublet can only have one of the following two C3 patterns:

We now verify this analytic conclusion numerically using the two toy models defined in Eqs. (S3.27) and (S3.30).
For both models, the band structure in the absence of the moiré potential is shown in Fig. S22(d). Qualitatively, it
can be viewed as two copies of the single-orbital-single-branch case shown in Fig. S22(a), giving rise to the sixfold-
degenerate state at KM and K′
M. When the moiré potential is maximized on the honeycomb sites, this sixfold state
splits into three doubly degenerate bands, as shown in Figs. S22(f) and S22(h), and the topmost isolated band set
carrying nonzero spin Chern number is topologically nontrivial. By contrast, when the moiré potential is maximized
on the triangular sites, the topmost isolated band set is topologically trivial and admits an atomic EBR description, as
shown in Figs. S22(e) and S22(g). The parameter values (mi, ai, bi, ci) in Eq. (S3.30) and (m′
i, a′

for each subfigure are listed in the corresponding caption. Thus, our analytic results in the previous paragraph are
verified by the numerical models.


## Page 44


44
c.
Γ-valley moire system III: layer group p312
In this section, we discuss the spin-U(1) -symmetric moiré system with time-reversal symmetric layer group p312,
generated by {C3z, C2x}. The following paragraphs are organized as follows. First, we show that the topmost isolate
band in the single-orbital-single-branch limit is required to be trivial as in the layer group p3 case. Second, we show
that the simple relation Eq. (S3.13) in the single-orbital-single-branch limit is lost once two orbitals/branches are
included, derive the general condition under which the topmost isolated band carries a nonzero C3z indicator, and
identify a band inversion between two blocks with different Πz as the route to tune the indicator. Finally, we use
the first-harmonic model to demonstrate numerically the phase transition between a trivial and a topological isolated
band.
We begin with the single-orbital-single-branch limit. For the k-dependent part, we consider up to O(k4),

the single-orbital-single-branch limit is lost once two orbitals/branches are included, and the topmost isolated band
is no longer forced to be trivial.
Since the topological property of the isolated band is parameter dependent, we now derive the condition under
which the isolated band set in layer group p312 can carry a nonzero C3z indicator for the spin Chern number. For a
single isolated band in spin sector s, the C3z symmetry-indicator formula reads

For both cases, a practical route to change the C3z indicator is therefore to tune the ordering of KM states through
a band inversion between two blocks with different Πz in one spin sector. Provided that the topmost state at ΓM is
unchanged during this process, once the gap reopens the inversion shifts Πz,1 (if in spin s) or Πz,2 (if in spin ¯s) to one
of the other two allowed values, changing Πz,1 −Πz,2 by ±1 (mod 3). Consequently, an isolated band that initially
carries zero spin Chern number acquires C↑= ±1 after the inversion, as dictated by Eqs. (S3.55) and (S3.58).

3.
Application to materials
Based on the analysis in Section III 2, we show that the emergence of an isolated topological band in twisted SnSe2
and ZrS2 originates from the inclusion of both bonding and antibonding branches and/or two effective orbitals.
a.

Now we analyze the moiré terms, (V1 −w2) is the first-harmonic term for the branch closer to the Fermi energy.
Because the sign of (V1 −w2) is negative as shown in Table S5, the moiré potential maximizes at honeycomb sites
and gives rise to the Dirac crossing in the single-orbital-single-branch limit, as discussed in Section III 2 b. This is
because the interlayer distance maximized at the honeycomb sites of the moiré unt cell as discussed in Section III 2 b.
Furthermore, V2 and w4 are inter-branch terms that break the single-orbital-single-branch limit. Specifically, the
w4 term breaks the effective ¯C2y symmetry represented by D(C2y) = τz ⊗s0. If we set w4 = 0, both spin-SU(2)
and the approximate ¯C2y emerge, forcing a four-fold degeneracy at KM and K′
M, as shown in Fig. S24(a). The
C3z eigenvalue of the four-fold degenerate state is ξKM,↑= ei π

2. For w4 ̸= 0, the effective C2y is broken and the top
band becomes isolated. As discussed in Section III 2 b, the isolated band split from the Dirac-like band represented
by a 2D EBR. The isolated band cannot be represented by any EBR with Jz = ± 3
2 and carries a nonzero spin Chern
number.

Appendix IV: Hartree-Fock and Exact Diagonalization Calculations
In this section, we investigate the interacting physics of AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer ZrS2, and AB-stacked
twisted bilayer SnSe2. In all cases, we will take the approximation that the non-interacting continuum model preserves
spin-U(1) symmetry

whose purpose is to set a reference point from which interactions are measured from. We note there has been no band

The interaction scheme corresponds to the choice of P ref. For the specific materials ZrS2 and SnSe2 treated here,
the monolayer band structure possesses a large ∼eV scale gap at charge neutrality between the valence and conduction
subspaces. Hence, a natural choice of reference density corresponds to fully occupying all valence bands. Since the
moiré continuum models of ZrS2 and SnSe2 capture just the valence degrees of freedom, P ref therefore consists of
fully occupying all the single-particle states within the plane-wave cutoff. This interaction scheme is analogous to
that used in K valley moiré TMDs such as twisted bilayer MoTe2 [87].
2.
Hartree-Fock results
To understand the interacting physics at integer filling factors ν of twisted bilayer ZrS2 and SnSe2, we perform
self-consistent Hartree-Fock (HF) calculations.
a.

difficulty. Before doing so, it is useful to first rewrite Htot in the single-particle band basis. The latter is expressed in
terms of the plane wave creation operators as

bands do not contribute to the physics of the active bands.

for AB-SnSe2, we use the U(1) -symmetric continuum model parameters extracted at θ = 4.41◦. White regions correspond

Light red and grey shading denote inter-spin coherence (ISC) with Chern number C = 1 and C = 0,
respectively. ‘QSHn’ denotes a quantum spin Hall phase with a spin Chern number of n. In the presence of finite ISC, ‘Z2 TI’
denotes a topological insulator with breaking spin-U(1) symmetry. System size is 12 × 12, screening length is dsc = 20 nm, and
we project to four valence moiré bands per spin.
= f↑↑(k)δs,↑δs′,↑δk,k′ + f↓↓(k)δs,↓δs′,↓δk,k′ + f↑↓(k)δs,↑δs′,↓δk+q,k′ + f↓↑(k)δs,↓δs′,↑δk,k′+q,
(S4.14)
where m, m′ are moiré band indices, and q is some moiré wavevector. In other words, we enforce moiré translation
symmetry, with the possible exception of inter-spin coherence (ISC) at wavevector q.
Note that ISC breaks the
spin-U(1) symmetry, which leads to non-zero f↑↓, f↓↑. We constrain q to the C3z-symmetric momenta ΓM, KM, K′
M.
We perform HF calculations with at least 25 initial seeds, and select the solution with the minimal HF energy. See
Ref. [88] for more details about solving the HF equations self-consistently.
b.
Phase diagrams: interaction strength vs twist angle
In Fig. S26, we present HF phase diagrams as a function of interaction strength 10/ϵr and twist angle θ. For a state
with non-vanishing Chern number (which hence breaks TRS), the sign of C can be flipped by applying TRS. We fix
the sign of C by imposing that the state has a net electron polarization in spin ↓(i.e. the doped holes for ν < 0 are
primarily in the spin ↑sector). We project to four moiré bands per spin for both ZrS2 and SnSe2.
We first discuss the results for ZrS2. We note that the phase diagrams for AA and AB stackings are similar. We
discuss the fillings ν = −1, −2, −4 in turn:
• At ν = −1 (left column of Fig. S26), we find a gapped phase for sufficiently strong interactions for both AA-ZrS2
and AB-ZrS2. The gapless region only has a small spin polarization, in contrast to the gapped region which
is fully spin polarized, except near the phase boundary where a small amount of ISC develops. The threshold

FIG. S27. Left: ν = −1 Hartree-Fock band structure of the C = 1 insulator for AA-ZrS2 using 10/ϵr = 1.6 and θ = 4.0◦. The
highest HF band (which is unoccupied) has > 99% overlap with the highest non-interacting band. We have shifted the energies
so that the highest state in the fully occupied spin ↓sector has E = 0. System size is 18 × 18, screening length is dsc = 20 nm,
and we project to four valence moiré bands per spin. Right: Corresponding non-interacting band structure.
interaction strength required to stabilize a gapped state grows with twist angle, which is consistent with the
increase in the non-interacting bandwidth of the top valence band. The gapped state is primarily a C = 1
insulator, where the unoccupied HF band has a high overlap with the highest non-interacting valence band in
one spin sector. For larger interaction strengths, we can observe a transition to a trivial C = 0 spin-polarized
insulator. The change in topology arises from a band inversion at the mBZ corner.
• At ν = −2 (middle column of Fig. S26), we find that both stackings of ZrS2 still require finite interactions to
yield an indirect gap, but the threshold interaction strength is smaller than that of ν = −1. Gapped symmetry-
preserving states are allowed at ν = −2, but exchange effects are still required to open an insulating gap due
to the indirect energy overlap between the first and second non-interacting moiré valence bands within each
spin. The first gapped phase we encounter from the metallic phase at large ϵr is a TRS-preserving topologically
non-trivial state. This is either a quantum spin Hall insulator (QSH1) with spin Chern number 1, or a Z2
topological insulator (TI) if there is a small amount of ISC which occurs near the phase boundary with the
metal. For larger interaction strengths, a significant ISC develops, and the system is primarily a C = 0 (C = 1)
insulator for AA (AB) stacking.
• At ν = −4 (right column of Fig. S26), gapped states are possible for both stackings in the non-interacting
limit at sufficiently small angles. This is because the second and third valence bands are already fully gapped
from each other at the non-interacting level, leading to a QSH3 with spin Chern number C = 3. For larger
interactions and twist angles, a band inversion between the second and third valence bands leads to a QSH1 or
Z2 TI.
In Fig. S27, we plot the HF band structure for a representative C = 1 state in AA-ZrS2 at θ = 4◦, and compare
it with the corresponding non-interacting dispersion. The HF bands in the spin ↓sector, which is fully occupied,
closely resembles the original non-interacting bands. This implies that the Hartree potential generated by the C = 1
state is not spatially inhomogeneous enough to reshape the dispersion. On the other hand, the unoccupied HF band
in spin ↑has been substantially renormalized by interactions. Most notably, for the interaction strength 10/ϵr = 1.6
considered here, bandwidth of the unoccupied HF band has been inflated to ∼110 meV compared to the original
value of ∼60 meV. This is driven by the enhancement of the energy difference between ΓM, which has a large Fock
self-energy, and KM, which has a smaller Fock self-energy. We note that the ΓM point has a smaller Berry curvature
and quantum geometry than the KM point. This can be connected to the momentum-contrasting Fock self-energy
according to the following argument. A significant Fock exchange between two HF orbitals requires that their Bloch
wavefunctions have similar layer and plane-wave structure, and that their momenta are close to each other (since
exchange is suppressed by the decay of the interaction potential). Since the region around KM has a large quantum
geometry, corresponding to rapidly varying layer/plane-wave structure, the Fock self-energy there is suppressed.
Finally, we briefly comment on the phase diagrams for AB-SnSe2. Since the first moiré valence band for twisted
SnSe2 is significantly narrower than that of twisted ZrS2, we find that gapped states begin to appear in AB-SnSe2 for
significantly smaller interaction strengths. The existence of a full indirect gap below the first band for sufficiently small
twist angles also means that symmetry-preserving gapped insulators are possible at ν = −2 in the non-interacting
limit, which would be a QSH1. However, for the interaction strengths 10/ϵr ≥0.4 considered, we do not find any
