c.
Phase diagrams: displacement field vs interaction strength
In Fig. S28, we show the phase diagram of θ = 3.89◦AA-ZrS2 and AB-ZrS2 as a function of displacement field
D and interaction strength. The displacement field is modelled as an energy difference D between the two layers in
the continuum model. At ν = −1, we find that D can drive a topological transition between the C = 1 and C = 0
insulators. At ν = −2, a large enough displacement field can destroy the QSH1/Z2 TI phase and lead to a gapless
state.
3.
Exact diagonalization results
We also perform a preliminary investigation into the possibility of fractional Chern insulators (FCIs) using exact
diagonalization (ED). In the following, we present results on θ = 3.89◦AA-ZrS2, but similar results are obtained for
AB-ZrS2 and AB-SnSe2. We consider the simplest scenario of a ν = −1/3 or ν = −2/3 spin-polarized FCI restricted
to the first non-interacting valence band. Hence, we do not include any potential band-mixing or spin depolarization
effects.
We recall from Section IV 2 b that interactions tend to enhance the band width of the first valence band (see
Fig. S27), which is primarily due to Fock exchange effects. Motivated by this observation, we apply an artificial
scaling factor αkin to the non-interacting dispersion in the calculation
E(k) →αkinE(k).
(S4.15)
Note that αkin = 1 is the physical limit, while αkin = 0 is the band-flattened limit. Negative αkin corresponds to
inverting the shape of the non-interacting dispersion. Our motivation is that inverting the kinetic dispersion may
counteract the interaction-induced renormalization, and hence lead to a narrow ‘effective’ band.
In the top left panels of Fig. S29, we show the results of ED calculations at ν = −1/3 and ν = −2/3 on 27- and
30-site lattices (see Fig. S29 right) for ϵr = 5. An FCI on the torus exhibits a characteristic topological degeneracy


## Page 55


55
27-site lattice
30-site lattice
FIG. S29. Calculations at fractional fillings in θ = 3.89◦AA-ZrS2 with dsc = 20 nm and ϵr = 5. Left: Top row shows the
FCI spread/gap ratio from exact diagonalization (ED) as a function of the scaling factor αkin for ν = −1/3 (left column) and
ν = −2/3 (right column). We consider αkin = −1.0, −0.8, . . . , 0.8, 1.0. Values of αkin without any data markers indicate the
absence of any FCI signatures. The ED calculation is restricted to the highest non-interacting valence band within a single spin
sector. Bottom row shows the effective mean-field bandwidth ν = −1/3 (left column) and ν = −2/3 (right column), computed
on a 12 × 12 mesh (see text for details). Right: Momentum meshes in the mBZ for the 27- and 30-site lattices.
whose momentum quantum numbers depend on the lattice choice. We diagnose the presence of an FCI by computing
the spread/gap ratio. To do this, we first identify the putative topological ground state manifold, which consists
of the lowest energy states with momenta consistent with the FCI momenta [29, 66]. The spread is defined as the
energy difference between the lowest and highest energy states in this topological ground state manifold. The gap is
defined as the energy difference between the lowest state not in this manifold, and the highest state in this manifold.
A well-formed FCI in finite-size should exhibit a spread/gap ratio which is small and positive. A large or negative
ratio indicates the absence of an FCI.
We find that an FCI only appears for a window of negative αkin for both ν = −1/3 and ν = −2/3. To understand
this, we plot an estimate of the ‘effective’ bandwidth in the bottom left panels of Fig. S29. This is computed by
constructing the following effective dispersion
Eeff.(k) = αkinE(k) + |ν|EHF,int(k).
(S4.16)
Above, EHF,int(k) is defined as the interacting part of the Hartree-Fock dispersion generated by fully hole-occupying
the valence band. Since this contribution is linear in the density matrix, we scale this by a factor |ν| to mimic the
effective contribution at fractional filling. We find that the position (as a function of αkin) of the minimum effective
bandwidth coincides well with the FCI region. We have checked other twist angles in the interval θ ∈[2.0◦, 6.0◦], and
found that the FCI only appears for negative αkin.
